MC	<p>db63_5f2a</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. MC	<p>d821_d1ae</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. MC	<p>9328_f5b3</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. MC	<p>2e65_8c12</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. MC	<p>fde0_c211</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. MC	<p>750e_3265</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. MC	<p>81d4_39c8</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. MC	<p>dd99_5241</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TTCCAG --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; border: 1px solid #999; display:inline-table;"> <tr><td style="border: 1px solid #999; padding: 2px 6px; text-align: center; font-family: monospace; font-size: 12pt;">5&prime;&ndash;</td><td style="border: 1px solid #999; padding: 2px 6px; text-align: center; font-family: monospace; font-size: 12pt;" bgcolor="#ffe6e6">&nbsp;<span style='color: #6e1212;'>T</span>&nbsp;</td> <td style="border: 1px solid #999; padding: 2px 6px; font-size: 12pt;">&nbsp;,&nbsp;</td> <td style="border: 1px solid #999; padding: 2px 6px; text-align: center; font-family: monospace; font-size: 12pt;" bgcolor="#e6f3ff">&nbsp;<span style='color: #003566;'>C</span>&nbsp;</td> <td style="border: 1px solid #999;
